## Emerging Europe

• Poland: NBP kept rates unchanged, as expected

• Türkiye: February CPI momentum calls for further rate hikes

## Poland: NBP kept rates on hold as expected

This week, the NBP decided to keep the policy rate unchanged at 5.75% (Figure 1), for a fifth consecutive time, as widely expected. All other parameters of the interest rate corridor were kept unchanged.

Figure 1: NBP key policy rate
![](images/0cd06ffc03c790bccdab54aa5b47b776f94a13201230672976c506e03e3bc8f6.jpg)

The statement gives a taste of the staff<sup>’</sup>s updated macro projections (which will be known in detail with the new Inflation Report). As usual, the commentary refers to the distribution of the forecast, not the central point: we are given the top and bottom of a central range of outcomes, to which the model attributes 50% probability. In summary, growth projections have been tweaked higher from November (Table 1), with the mid-point of the forecast range revised higher by 0.7%-pts in both 2024 and 2025 (to 3.5% and 4.3%, respectively). For inflation on the contrary, the numbers are much lower, especially in 2024, where the mid-point is down 1.2%-pt to 3.6%.

Table 1: NBP forecast<sup>1</sup> %oya
<table><tr><td colspan="3"></td><td colspan="3"></td><td colspan="3">CPI</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2"></td><td>23</td><td>24</td><td></td><td>…………… 25</td><td>23</td><td>24</td><td>25</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="3">Nov 2023</td><td>range low</td><td>-0.1</td><td>1.9</td><td></td><td>2.4 …1…</td><td>11.3</td><td>3.2</td><td>2.2</td></tr><tr><td>range high</td><td>0.6</td><td>3.8</td><td></td><td>4.7</td><td>11.5</td><td>6.2</td><td>5.3</td></tr><tr><td>mid-point</td><td>0.3</td><td>2.9</td><td></td><td>3.6</td><td>11.4</td><td>4.7</td><td>3.8</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="3">Mar 2024</td><td>range low</td><td></td><td>2.7</td><td></td><td>3.2</td><td></td><td>2.8</td><td>2.2</td></tr><tr><td>range high</td><td></td><td>4.3</td><td></td><td>5.3</td><td></td><td>4.3</td><td>5.0</td></tr><tr><td>mid-point</td><td></td><td>3.5</td><td></td><td>4.3</td><td></td><td>3.6</td><td>3.6</td></tr><tr><td>+/- mid-point</td><td></td><td></td><td>0.7</td><td></td><td>0.7</td><td></td><td>-1.2</td><td>-0.2</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">memo:</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">J.P. Morgan</td><td></td><td>0.2</td><td>2.8</td><td>2.7</td><td>11.6</td><td>4.2</td><td>4.7</td></tr></table>

1 The NBP statement presents forecasts as a distribution rather than a point. The projection attributes 50% probability the variable will be within the low-high range Source: : NBP, J.P. Morgan

However, in the statement we learn that because the projection is built based on the assumption of no changes to food VAT or administrative limits on the prices of energy, the “balance of risk for inflation is markedly asymmetric<sup>”</sup>. Specifically, “in the case of 2024, the probability that annual price growth will run above the 50-percent confidence interval is 43%, while the probability that it will run below this range is 7%.<sup>”</sup> This massive skew in the distribution means the midpoint is almost a best-case scenario, and rather incomparable to the previous projection. The real modal view is more likely closer to the top limit of the range (4.3%), which imp]lies a much smaller revision.

The press release describes a picture of continued disinflation which has everything going for it: unwinding supply chocks, lower cost pressures, subdued economic growth and Zloty strength. However, according to the statement, the big chal lenge is that the outlook is characterized by “substantial uncertainty<sup>”</sup>, in particular relating to food VAT, regulated energy prices, and a potential acceleration of demand pressures (due to public sector wage increases) all of which can trigger an increase in inflation.

## Türkiye: Feb CPI calls for further rate hikes

Headline CPI inflation came in at 4.5% m/m nsa in February, much higher than our expectation of 4.2% and the market consensus of 3.8%. CPI inflation increased to 67.1% oya in February, from 64.9% in January, mainly driven by services prices. Services prices rose by a high 5.7% m/m in February, leading over-year-ago services inflation to an all-time high of 94.4%. Core inflation (C Index; CPI excluding energy, food, beverages, tobacco and gold) rose by a high 3.6% m/m, leading oya core inflation to 72.9% in February, from 70.5% oya in January (Figure 2).

![](images/65b2ba32b5ad563171e5269f46934667620a90e630afdbb42ad7376967fc7ad8.jpg)
Core inflation momentum (3m change, saar, %) continued to accelerate in February mainly due to services prices. Core goods inflation momentum also increased albeit to a lesser extent (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Inflation momentum indicators 3m change, saar, %
![](images/cee4cc0c30825827c7d5a433027f894a42f7310ebe708fe72995038256276de1.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan, TurkStat

Headline over-year-ago inflation is set to rise towards 76% in May 2024 (previously 73%) owing to higher-than-expected wage hikes and base effects driven by natural gas prices. We revise up our year-end inflation forecast to 44.7% (previously 42.2%), due to stickier than expected services prices (Figure 4).

Figure 4: CPI inflation
![](images/6f29d902808eb63a53980fc740358214e9be84bd0caf91be9aac5c7f1ba117da.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan, TurkStat

The February data align with our view that the current monetary and fiscal policy mix is not tight enough to reduce inflation. The CBRT kept the door open for further rate hikes should CPI inflation deviate from forecast on the upside, and that is what the February CPI have done. CPI is likely to be above 3% m/m in March. Hence we now expect the CBRT to hike its policy rate by 500bp to 50% in April (previously 45%). We also expect macroprudential measures from the CBRT to increase Turkish lira deposit rates to stop rising dollarization among Turkish residents. We maintain our year-end policy rate forecast at 45% as we expect the CBRT to cut its policy rate by 250bp in November and December.

0.7%m/m, but the over-year-ago should slow to 7.8% from 8.2%.

## South Africa

• GDP partially recovered with 0.1%q/q sa growth in 4Q23

CAD forecast at 2.7% of GDP this year (previously 2.4%) after an unexpected 4Q23 widening to 2.3%

• Spotlight shifts to politics ahead of May 29 elections

While the economy avoided a recession in 4Q, overall GDP growth weakened from 1.9%y/y in 2022 to 0.6% last year. Unprecedented power cuts, significant logistical challenges, and an income squeeze drove the slowdown in economic activity. Agriculture led the slowdown with output recording a 29-year low with a 12.2%y/y contraction last year. Indeed, the sector is impacted by electricity cuts as well as by the logistical challenges. The impact of these likely moderate this year as the intensity of power cuts reduces, while reforms in the rail sector slowly begin to take shape.

GDP growth staged a partial recovery in 4Q23 with a 0.1%q/ q increase, from a 0.2% fall, as negative effects from the agricultural sector were stronger than we anticipated (Figure 1). Agriculture extended its declining streak with a 9.7%q/q (33.5% ar) drop after 11.7%q/q contraction in 3Q. This shaved off 0.2%-pt of GDP, despite high-frequency data painting a positive picture in recent months. Outside agriculture, outcomes were broadly as expected with an IP rebound. Meanwhile, the jump in employee compensation to 6.8%oya in 4Q, from 5.1%, combined with the likely easing in purchasing power squeeze this year should shape a somewhat better outlook for consumption.

Figure 1: South Africa - Contributions to GDP growth
![](images/e15d175c0f924119f133c403bdb1bbd8c4dfc7d1d5fd6017e7f348b491d54716.jpg)
Source: StatsSA. J.P. Morgan calculations

Separately, a strong recovery in import volumes compressed the trade surplus in 4Q, resulting in the current account deficit expanding to 2.3% of GDP. The deterioration was mainly due to the goods sector as the trade balance surplus shrank to 1.2% of GDP in 4Q23, from 2.6% previously. A marginal deterioration in the terms of trade coincided with a solid 4%q/ q rise in import volumes, following an 8.7% decline in 3Q23. Yet, an unexpected widening in invisibles deficit (income,

services and transfers) to 3.6% of GDP (from 3.1%) drove the miss relative to our estimate of a 1.8% CAD. We project the CAD to widen to 2.7% of GDP this year as the trade surplus shrinks, while the invisibles gap remains elevated at around 3%.

## Opinion polls point to tight election

Near term focus will be on upcoming elections on 29 May 2024. Opinion polls suggest the ANC could lose its outright majority. Support for the ANC has dropped at an 8%pts per electoral cycle at the local government level, reaching 45.6% in 2021. A similar development at national level may necessitate some form of a coalition agreement (either formal or a confidence-and-supply arrangement) with other parties, adding uncertainty to the policy outlook. Coalition scenarios probably hinge on the level of ANC support. In a range of 45%-49% we expect relatively few policy concessions, while at 41% and below uncertainty about the political arrangement is high (Table 1). Policy priorities of other parties differ considerably, with the EFF describing itself as a revolutionary socialist economic emancipation movement.

Table: Election scenarios
<table><tr><td>Support for ANC (% of votes)</td><td>Political alliances</td><td>Policy outlook</td></tr><tr><td>50% and above</td><td>ANC holds legislative majority, no arrangement with other parties</td><td>Policy continuity, socio-economic pressures present</td></tr><tr><td>45%-49%</td><td>ANC teams up with smaller parties</td><td>Policy continuity with few policy compromises</td></tr><tr><td>42%-44%</td><td>High uncertainty on political arrangements: Many small parties;arrangements with EFF/DA</td><td>Partnership with smaller parties has policy continuity; DA focused on governance; EFF states it pursues radical socialist objectives</td></tr><tr><td>41% and below</td><td>Increased need for formal alliance with EFF or DA; diminished scope for smaller parties only</td><td>Greater reliance on main opposition parties, more policy compromises; outlook hinges on whether EFFor DA</td></tr></table>

Source: J.P.Morgan, EFF election manifesto, ANC election manifesto

A key market concern is that emerging opposition parties (e.g. EFF) argue for a more radical and left-leaning approach. Coalition with such parties could therefore risk significant policy concessions from the ANC. Much focus is on the EFF, which considers itself a revolutionary socialist movement. As outlined in its election manifesto, the party continues to strive for the expropriation of land without compensation by means of amending section 25 of the constitution. Moreover, it seeks to cap interest rates, double the existing grants and advocate for state-led industrialization and expanded SOEs to drive employment. Surveys place EFF support around 15%-20% of votes, somewhat behind the official opposition DA.

## Australia and New Zealand

• Australian 4Q real GDP rose 0.2%q/q sa, meeting consensus and RBA forecasts

• The current account improved on the back of stronger services trade and lower income debits

• Building approvals are still depressed and indicate dwelling supply remains challenged

Australia's 4Q national accounts data landed in line with our/ consensus forecasts of +0.2%q/q (not annualized), expectations which had been trimmed slightly after the last week<sup>’</sup>s worth of partial data. There was also an upward revision to 3Q (+0.3%q/q from 0.2%q/q) though not sufficient to make the annual rate surprise, with the economy ending the year at 1.5%oya growth. We have held the view that 2H23 was the sequential trough for growth, mostly given the outlook for households, and the detail seems to be relatively supportive.

While consumption skidded along the bottom near 3Q's pace, income growth firmed in the quarter and the saving rate unexpectedly rose from 1.9% to 3.2% (Figure 1). Compensation of employees lifted a firm 1.4%q/q, some of which came from temporary support via extra public-sector earnings from administration of the referendum. The latter will drop out into 1H, but households will gain offsetting real income support from the considerable drop in the pace of inflation, and removal of the incremental rate hike headwind which was so intense over the prior 18 months. This will be complemented by tax cuts in 2H. With the saving rate having some prospect of absorbing any future drags (RBA forecast for the 4Q23 saving rate had been a considerably lower +0.9%), the board should have reasonable confidence that momentum is stabilizing into 2024.

Figure 1: Australian household saving rate % of disposable income
![](images/1185d9e31c3323fc187fffb7c1f4f3722460a13923fa6466ef5fa6a6c661f91b.jpg)

## GDP and labor market interactions

The economy's much bemoaned productivity performance seems to have turned the corner with an +0.5%q/q increase in GDP per hour worked, and has strung together a 3% annualized gain over the second half. This is a strong performance and is demonstrating the usual countercyclical/hours-based property seen through the Australian business cycle. As argued previously, this inevitable bounce (see here and here) should mean productivity outcomes will move into the background and are not a constraint for monetary policy

Domestic demand grew in line with GDP, but over the last year as a whole the relative strength of final demand for imports has meant domestic demand (+2.3%oya) is holding up better than GDP (+1.5%oya, chart). We typically think of domestic demand as being a better arbiter for the labor market, so this space bears watching. The leading indicators in vacancies/job advertisements, etc. appear to have stabilized somewhat around the turn of the year, after consistent softening.

## Current account looking a little healthier

The current account surplus rebounded to A\$11.8 billion (J.P. Morgan: A\$5.5 billion, consensus: A\$5 billion), while the prior quarter<sup>’</sup>s deficit was revised higher to reflect a modest surplus of A\$1.3 billion. The upside surprise in the current account owed to a combination of stronger services trade (this data is no longer reported monthly) and a reduction in the income deficit. The reduction in the income deficit was mostly a function of a decline in income derived from FDI paid to foreign asset holders. While income debits have trended lower since 2022, in level terms payments to non-resident holders of Australian assets remain close to record highs (Figure 2), reflecting a surge in offshore investment into both domestic equity and bond markets from 2020

Figure 2: Australian primary income credits and debits A\$bn, quarterly
![](images/2d0c7dabd72ed167f66d796278be2fb8de93e3a1771c3b9b8058114818f56961.jpg)

## Building supply remains challenged

January<sup>’</sup>s building approvals data disappointed with total approval volumes falling 1%m/m, while the prior month<sup>’</sup>s outcome was revised lower (-10.1%m/m). The headline print masks some wild underlying swings as a double-digit decline in detached dwelling approvals (-10%m/m) was largely offset by a spike in the higher density sector (+19.5%m/m). In prior years, the ABS has noted that staff shortages over the holiday period (also evidenced in total hours worked in the labor

force survey) have slowed the administrative process and created a backlog of approvals. The spike in survey respondents reporting marginal attachment and sharp decline in hours evident in January<sup>’</sup>s labor force survey hints at a similar dynamic at play in early 2024, and we expect a rebound in approval volumes in upcoming prints. However, even accounting for seasonal distortions, building approval volumes remain at multi year lows and indicate the near-term supply outlook remains challenged (Figure 3), particularly in the context of strong population growth.

Figure 3: Australian residential building approval volumes
![](images/ebbcdaad7616a37c3a9fbf916715b70adac6b4a2b1e8cd6b17c89e0d6460485a.jpg)
Source: ABS, J.P. Morgan

## Mortgage activity slipping

Australian mortgage lending for January was soft, following a 4.1%m/m contraction in December with a 3.9%m/m fall, below our/consensus forecast of +2.0%m/m. The deterioration was spread across owner-occupier (-4.6%m/m) and investor (-2.6%m/m) loan flows in value terms. Dwelling prices have also lost momentum lately, consistent with the earlier signal from leading indicators like transaction volumes and auction clearance rates. The slower rate of dwelling price appreciation represents convergence back toward a pace more consistent with subdued trends in mortgage activity.

The %3m/3m rate on owner-occupier lending has dropped further (-34% ar) and to a lesser extent in investor mortgages (-10% 3m/3m ar), similar to the pullback seen in late 2022 when the cash rate was rising steeply. Though this looks weak, the cycle for gross lending flows tends to be of higher frequency and wider amplitude than that for credit growth (changes in the stock of debt) and the latter matters more for house price performance and consumption. On the basis of the lending data, credit growth should stay relatively stable at a pace in line with trend national income growth. If so, house prices and credit growth should be a relatively neutral factor for the policy outlook.

## Greater China

• China: 2024 NPC takeaways

• Strong growth in Jan-Feb exports

• FX reserves beat on strong exports

• Taiwan: February CPI inflation picked up to 3.1%oya, reflecting LNY seasonality

• February exports moderated as expected; look for steady export growth in 2024

Premier Li Qiang delivered his first Government Work Report to the NPC. The major numerical targets for this year are broadly in line with our expectations. The annual growth target is set at “around 5%<sup>”</sup> (in line with our expectation, Figure 1). For the fiscal deficit target, the official budgetary deficit is 4.06 trillion yuan (3% of GDP) plus 1 trillion yuan (0.7% of GDP) special central government bond (vs. our expectation of 3.8% of GDP). The quota for special local government bond is 3.9 trillion yuan (vs 3.8 trillion yuan last year, and our forecast of 3.5 trillion yuan).

Figure 1: China real GDP growth vs. government's growth target
![](images/1493edba782feb79ffbfdbef45948d39a578003f8b8baaa3b1cc30f5a591e9b8.jpg)
Source: NBS, J.P. Morgan

The fiscal budget report adopts the similar approach of “3% of GDP + 1 trillion yuan<sup>”</sup> as last year, making the total at 3.7% of GDP (using the MOF budgetary report implied nominal GDP). Considering the carryover of the 500 billion yuan additional CGB issued in 4Q23, the actual budgetary deficit will increase from 3.5% of GDP in 2023 to 4.1% in 2024. Premier Li also announced a multi-year ultra-long central government bond arrangement with 1 trillion yuan for this year, to support construction of major strategic projects, etc.

The quota for special local government bonds is set at 3.9 trillion yuan, higher than our forecast of 3.5 trillion. We have argued that the range of 3.5-4.0 trillion should be reasonable, and a higher quota will increase the room for tightening on LGFV borrowing. Taking into account the adjustment in other components, we expect the augmented fiscal deficit will increase by 0.2% of GDP this year to 12.2% of GDP, roughly the same as our previous forecast (+0.1% of GDP).

There is no additional lift of the debt ceiling for local governments. Local government debt limit is raised by 4620bn yuan to 46.8 trillion yuan, with 720bn yuan for general local government debt, and 3900bn yuan for special local government debt. Both are equivalent to the local government<sup>’</sup>s budgetary deficit target and special LGB quota for this year. Hence, we do not see additional room for local government debt resolution.

The inflation target was set at 3% (a ceiling concept in practice). It will not be binding as our forecast of average CPI this year is 0.6%. The monetary policy tone is largely in line with that set at the CEWC last December. We maintain our forecast of a continuation of accommodative interest rate policy and credit policy for 2024. After the larger-than-expected 25bp reduction in 5-year LPR, our baseline forecast still looks for two 10bp policy rate cuts, with the next rate cut anticipated in the current month. If it happens, we look for 10bp cut for 1-year LPR, but no change for 5-year LPR.

Total government bond issuance likely will exceed 10 trillion if we have additional special local government refinancing bonds issuance. This will comprise the official deficit at 4.06 trillion yuan, which will be funded by the issuance of general central and local government bonds, 3.9 trillion yuan special local government bonds, and 1 trillion yuan ultra-long central government bond. We maintain our call for another 25bp cut for RRR in 2H, to ensure ample and stable liquidity conditions, though it can be replaced by expanded MLF operation.

For structural monetary policy, we maintain our forecast for a 1 trillion yuan package supported by the PBOC<sup>’</sup>s PSL operation to support public housing, urban village renovation and public infrastructure. The Government Work Report does mention an increase in construction and supply of affordable housing. This 1 trillion yuan assumption underscores our relatively constructive growth forecast for this year, which is echoed by the 500bn yuan PSL issuance in December and January. With that, we estimate the real estate investment contraction will narrow from 8-10% to 2-4%, lifting economic growth by around 0.5%pt.

The government work report laid out 10 major tasks for this year. On promoting industrial renovations and TFP, the report emphasized the importance of advanced manufacturing, the upgrade / transformation of traditional manufacturing sectors and development of producing-type service sectors. On promoting domestic demand, the report emphasized the need to increase household income, improve demand and remove restrictive measures, to encourage trade-in consumption for NEVs and electronic products, to support elderly care, childcare and housekeeping services, etc. On housing policy, the report reiterated recent policy guidelines to meet the reasonable funding demand from real estate developers irrespective of ownership structure, to ensure stable development in the housing market, and to accelerate Hukou reform and citizenship of migrant workers. The report also emphasized equal treatment between SOEs and non-SOEs, national treatment for foreign companies and some practical issues to encourage foreign investments.

The NPC announced that Premier Li will not host a press conference on the closing day of the NPC, and the press conference will also be canceled going forward. This is a practice that started in 1988 and has been institutionalized since 1993. The announcement came in as a surprise, and it is disappointing, in our view. Especially at this time when the government and investors have contrasting narratives on China<sup>’</sup>s current economic conditions, the desirable policy responses and economic outlook, it is important and helpful for the government to improve policy communications and clarify its policy framework.

## Strong growth in Jan-Feb exports

China<sup>’</sup>s export activity beat expectations significantly in January-February (Figure 2). Merchandise exports (in USD terms) showed strong growth of 7.1%oya in Jan-Feb, while imports also came in moderately above expectations, rising 3.5%oya. In the breakdown, low-end consumer goods exports continued to outperform, while exports of mechanical and electrical products as well as high-end products also showed solid growth. Strong export growth was broad-based across major markets, including the EU, the US, ASEAN and Taiwan. Exports to Latam and Africa also showed strong growth momentum. On the import side, processing imports showed significant growth in Jan-Feb, while major commodity imports (crude oil, iron ore, copper) showed somewhat mixed performance.

Figure 2: China merchandise exports and imports
![](images/7ae7498c74c6f2d0a7192f419a7262cc67dc3843a7f5dad1a90d743ed6f1413e.jpg)
2012 2013 2014 2015 2016 2017 2018 2019 2020 2021 2022 2023 2024 Source: China Customs, J.P. Morgan

Looking back, China<sup>’</sup>s export activity generally held up better than expected in 2023, benefiting from its diversification efforts across major markets (EM Asia, LatAm, Russia and Africa) and by products (with rapid expansion in new energy products and motor vehicles). Going into 1Q24, the strong

Jan-Feb export growth came on the back of constructive signals from a few recent indicators, including China<sup>’</sup>s manufacturing PMI, latest signals from alt-data, and positive signals from other Asian exports (including Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s export orders report).

We look for relatively steady growth in China<sup>’</sup>s exports though 2024 (with some possible payback to the significant Jan-Feb strength in the coming months) amid a steady global growth outlook. Following solid manufacturing FAI growth and capacity expansion in the past two years, and amid lingering softness in domestic demand conditions, Chinese exporters will continue to push for expanding their global market share. Trade partners<sup>’</sup> potential reactions will however become a growing concern this year.

## FX reserves beat on strong exports

China<sup>’</sup>s February FX reserves surprised on the upside, rising US\$6.5bn to \$3225.8bn (Figure 3). While China Customs has not published its official breakdown for Jan and Feb, considering the working-day difference, we estimate US\$68.8bn trade surplus for Jan, and \$56.3bn for Feb. Note that SAFE<sup>’</sup>s January current account surplus came in at US\$34.6bn, which may have been followed by a marginal narrowing to \$33.3bn in February. The US dollar index inched up from 103.3 to 104.2 in February, leading to an estimated currency valuation loss of US\$11.6bn. We estimate an implied capital outflow at US\$15.2bn, compared to the revised estimate of US\$27.2bn outflow for January.

Figure 3: China monthly change in FX reserves USD bn
![](images/663ede77d1035cf11eccc050b4d3239569f52a15d572c96458deb0d382a3e84e.jpg)

From November 2022 to February 2024, China has increased its gold reserves by 9.94mn oz, or up by 15.9%. In the past week, gold prices surged to a record high after staying largely stable in the first two months of the year. It is partly due to the rising attraction of gold as a non-yielding asset when the market prices in a closer rate cut. It was likely also affected by large-scale gold futures purchase last week and solid retail demand in China amid the sluggish housing and underperforming equity market. However, underlying purchase motivation of central banks<sup>’</sup> gold reserves accumulation is more about FX reserves diversification, risk mitigation, inflation

hedging, economic and financial stability, etc. In this case, despite the recent gold price surge, we think gold reserves likely will continue to increase, both in China and globally.

## Taiwan: Feb CPI inflation picked up

Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s February CPI inflation picked up to 3.08%oya (Figure 4), partly reflecting Lunar New Year-related seasonal factors. The details suggest a modest gain in food prices, marginal decline in fuel prices, with significant (LNY-related) uptick in core CPI.

Figure 4: Taiwan headline and core CPI %oya
![](images/59a5027e376315d37fa4c54c477b5cbdd5e10dd47f8f7d2e7a31780164a1aa89.jpg)
2011 2012 2013 2014 2015 2016 2017 2018 2019 2020 2021 2022 2023 2024 Source: DGBAS, J..P. Morgan

With muted imported inflation, domestic consumer spending losing momentum, and easing employment growth, we expect Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s headline CPI inflation to gradually ease going forward. CPI inflation will likely stay above 2% through early 3Q24, and moderate to below 2% towards late 3Q-4Q. Overall, we expect headline CPI inflation to average 2.1% in 2024, while core CPI inflation will likely average at 2.0%. Regarding the growth picture, we expect the Taiwan economy to show moderate growth, with average 2.2% q/q saar sequential growth in 1H24 and 2.6% q/q saar in 2H24, with full-year 2024 GDP growth picking up to 4.3%yoy given the low base last year.

## Expecting policy rates on hold on Mar 21<sup>st</sup>

Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s central bank delivered a rather modest 75bp hike in the latest round of the global rate hike cycle through 1Q23. Looking ahead, our US colleagues expect the Fed to start its rate cut cycle in June. We doubt the Taiwan central bank will rush to follow suit, as Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s rate increase has been rather modest, and we expect headline CPI to stay above 2% in the near term, with real interest rates staying below the historical average. We expect Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s central bank to keep policy rates on hold in their upcoming monetary policy meeting on March 21<sup>st</sup>.

## Feb exports moderated as expected

Following the strong sequential growth trend in 4Q, Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s Jan-Feb trade reports show some moderation in export activity. February merchandise exports came in line with expectations, rising 1.3%oya. Seasonally adjusted, we estimate that exports (in volume terms) fell 4.0% m/m sa in February, adding to the decline of 3.1% m/m sa in January, and growing 6.4% 3m/3m saar. Overall tech exports fell 2.2% m/m sa and 2.4% m/m sa in February and January respectively, following strong growth in 4Q (Figure 5). Further breakdown highlights strong underlying expansion trend in exports of information and communication products (+5.5% m/m sa in February, or up 101.5% 3m/3m saar), likely reflecting strong growth in exports of AI-related products. Breakdown by export destinations highlights ongoing outperformance in exports to the US (+106.4% 3m/3m saar).

Figure 5: Taiwan export breakdown by products - level US\$ bn, sa, 3mma
![](images/536b1c04d232704ce1667ff0d9a1d3baa308417fcf772c056cf5d6e7afaea8f8.jpg)

Imports showed notable near term volatility, falling 20.0% m/ m sa in February, after the jump of 21.1% m/m sa in January, possibly reflecting LNY-related volatility. Capital goods imports seem to show some signs of stabilization, suggesting the persistent capex weakness in recent quarters may have bottomed. Going forward, we look for moderate growth in tech exports in the coming quarters, including structural boost of AI-related products and HPC- related demand, while the tech sector<sup>’</sup>s de-stocking pressure begins to ease.

<table><tr><td>US$ bn</td><td colspan="4"></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Nov</td><td>Dec</td><td>Jan-Feb</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Balance</td><td>69.5</td><td>75.3</td><td>110.0</td><td>125.2</td></tr><tr><td>Exports</td><td>292.7</td><td>303.6</td><td>507.7</td><td>528.1</td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>0.5</td><td>2.3</td><td>0.3</td><td>7.1</td></tr><tr><td>Imports</td><td>223.3</td><td>228.3</td><td>397.8</td><td>402.9</td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>-0.6</td><td>0.2</td><td>21</td><td>3.5</td></tr></table>

China<sup>’</sup>s export activity beat expectations significantly in January-February. Merchandise exports (in USD terms) showed strong growth of 7.1%oya in Jan-Feb, compared to the moderate rise of 2.3%oya in December. In sequential terms, we estimate that average Jan-Feb exports jumped 7.5% m/m sa compared to the December level. Imports also came in moderately above expectations, rising 3.5%oya, following growth of 0.2%oya in December. Seasonally adjusted, we estimate average Jan-Feb nominal imports increased 1.6%m/m sa from the December level. As a result,the trade surplus came in at US\$125.2 billion in Jan-Feb, compared to \$75.3bn in December (and \$823.2 billion for full-year 2023).

## Korea

• Headline inflation accelerated temporarily in Feb

• IP fell in January on a pause in chip manufacturing

• Manufacturing PMI edged down from a 1.5-year high

We have long expected that the net exports (thus the manufacturing sector) should lead the GDP growth recovery and CPI inflation should gradually normalize through 2024. At first glance, this week<sup>’</sup>s data points are at odds with that expectation. January IP fell, suggesting a moderation of IP growth in 1Q. And February CPI inflation accelerated in over-year-ago terms. However, the projected slowing in trend IP growth and the monthly fall of manufacturing PMI are modest. CPI inflation should remain on a gradually stabilizing path. And tech sector recovery should continue to bolster GDP growth, and domestic demand<sup>’</sup>s softness should contribute to inflation stability by 2H this year.

## Headline inflation accelerated temporarily

Headline inflation accelerated as expected in February to 3.1%oya (JPMe and consensus 3.0%) from 2.8% in January. Seasonally adjusted, headline CPI rose 0.4%m/m, while core CPI rose 0.2%, with the headline monthly gain mainly driven by agricultural prices. But in trend terms, headline CPI<sup>’</sup>s trend moderated further to 1.4%3m/3m, saar, from 5.3% in October 2023, and core CPI<sup>’</sup>s also moderated further to 1.6% (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Consumer prices %3m/3m, saar
![](images/8dd28cba639129fe827b8f0c2b241876c7227c34ef98c55edab5230019244e2c.jpg)
Source: NSO and J.P. Morgan

Farm product prices rose 2.1%m/m, sa, for the tenth consecutive monthly rise and stronger than in the past three months (0.1%\~0.7%). They have risen 11.4% over a year ago. Especially, fresh fruit prices jumped up by 7.6%m/m, sa. While it is hard to make a near-term forecast on this volatile category, cost-push from imported energy prices, climate change impacts which are unfavorable for local fresh food production, and labor shortages in rural areas could be potential causes. That said, the monthly rise in February was within the usual volatility of farm product prices, and pressures from energy costs have likely passed the peak.

Meanwhile, core inflation is trending lower. Non-oil industrial goods prices rose 0.1%m/m, sa, and 2.0%3m/3m, saar. Service prices also rose modestly by 0.13%m/m, sa, and 1.9%3m/3m, saar, to suggest more stable underlying price pressure in core categories. Another round of cost spillover to core prices is an upside risk (i.e. agricultural prices<sup>’</sup> spillover to restaurant prices), yet the soft demand-side condition should continue to dampen core price pressure, in our view.

Looking forward, we continue to expect that the headline over-year-ago inflation should approach the 2% target by 3Q24 to follow the lead by the stabler core price inflation, yet the annual inflation forecast tracks a bit higher at 2.6%y/y than our previous expectation (2.4%). We have two more monthly CPI data prints before the Bank of Korea<sup>’</sup>s forecast revision in May and we will need to wait for more clarity, but it now seems that the risk is balanced around the current BoK forecast for 2024 inflation at 2.6%.

## IP fell in January on a pause in chip making

IP decreased by 1.3%m/m, sa, in January, for the third monthly drop in the past four months. That said, this was a correction after solid gains in August-September, and its level in January was still higher than the 3Q23 average. Its sequential growth decelerated to 1.0% 3m/3m, saar, from the peak of 16.8% in November and 8.8% in December (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Industrial production %3m/3m, saar, both axes
![](images/9c253e7ee38902b18a134abb1e9eac139150179c1e186958dfdcca0a70308b43.jpg)
Source: NSO and J.P. Morgan

Tech remained a key determinant of overall IP growth, as the sector<sup>’</sup>s production fell 4.8%m/m in January, after rising sharply in August-September and hovering near 1-1/2-year highs over the prior three months. Semiconductor production fell 8.6% in January after solid gains in November-December, due to the seasonality where production and shipments are concentrated in the end of each quarter. Meanwhile, non-tech production fell for four months in the past five, so the divergence between resilient tech (+15.1%3m/3m, saar) vs. anemic non-tech production (5.1% contraction) continued in January.

## PMI still above average despite decline

Manufacturing PMI fell by 0.5pt to 50.7 in February, partly reversing a 1.3pt gain in January, though remaining the second-highest in the past 1.5 years. The output PMI implies IP growth of a mid-to-high single-digit annual rate (Figure 3). Going forward, IP growth may continue to moderate, as base effects fade in the tech sector after the sector<sup>’</sup>s robust rebound in 2H23, but the above-average output PMI suggests that IP will maintain a decent pace despite some slowdown.

Figure 3: Korea manufacturing output - PMI vs. IP

## ASEAN

• Singapore<sup>’</sup>s January production softened on tech, demand remains resilient

Domestic demand growth stepped down in December and January, discretionary spending softened

• 2024 budget provides limited fiscal impulse

• January core CPI surprised to the downside, February in focus, pipeline pressures ease

Singapore<sup>’</sup>s data has been soft of late, with January manufacturing contracting sequentially, December retail and food and beverage spending slowing and January resident airport departures unusually soft. However, we are mindful that Lunar New Year (LNY) distortions, with the ensuing decline in the signal to noise ratio, suggest caution in reading too much into data. In this context, we expect that external demand – especially in tech – remains buoyant and should be evident over the course of this quarter. However, the softening trend in private consumption has been in place since 3Q23. Moreover, we expect that labor income growth will slow and put a lid on discretionary spending over the course of this year. While the budget has provisioned for some assistance for lower income households, the overall fiscal impulse is expected to be neutral.

The same easing in labor incomes evident in the 4Q23 unit labor cost data should reduce pipeline price pressures. However, we are mindful that accumulated and latent costs remain and could manifest in core prices in the coming months, especially around LNY. Thus, while we are of the view that inflation should be on a gradual path softer, each data release should be watched for a broadening in price pressures. We maintain our view that MAS policy should be on hold this year, but remain watchful of these risks.

## A mixed picture of demand; softer inside

Singapore<sup>’</sup>s January manufacturing production came in weaker than expected, up 1.1%oya (Consensus: 4.3%oya, J.P. Morgan: 1.8%oya) and down 5.7%m/m, sa, marking a reversal of the strong 4Q23 outturn (Figure 1). By sector, tech output drove a large part of the decline, down 16.8%m/m, sa, especially in semiconductors, while non-tech rose 9.6%m/m, sa, and it remains to be seen whether the non-tech lift continues.

Although the tech uplift in Singapore during 4Q23 aligns with the lift in the tech bellwethers of Korea and Taiwan, tech output in Singapore has tended to be more volatile. With the incoming tech data from N. Asia remaining constructive, we would expect a similar trajectory in Singapore tech output as well, with the January tech contraction reflecting technical payback rather than a change in trend. However, outside of tech, the levels of production have been broadly moving sideways and suggest limited spillover into the non-tech sectors so far, though the January rise does provide some hope of a sectorial broadening.

We expect external demand to be resilient premised on the lift in tech, but domestic demand growth appears to be slowing. Passenger flight data report an ongoing recovery in non-resident travel while resident slowed in January (Figure 2). The sharp drop in restaurant spending is consistent with a slowdown in discretionary spending (Figures 3 and 4). Similarly, retail sales and food and beverage spending has been slowing since 3Q23; the slowdown in recreational spending could be post-COVID related normalization.

Figure 1: Singapore manufacturing production Index, 2015=100, sa
![](images/8d79afe64981948e13bc39906a0daac0d448f034d5906e3256d9bd0dc991be76.jpg)

Figure 2: Singapore Changi airport departures
![](images/6ce9d8bc0fed0aa31a7b050776433fa7c0c464a5f151167987e556c5819e113d.jpg)

Figure 3: Singapore retail sales ex. motor vehicles Index, 2017=100, sa, real terms
![](images/f48847e87ed9317f401318b5670053235ded8d5636fbdd32d55b14011da74902.jpg)

Figure 4: Singapore food and beverage sales Index, 2017=100, sa, real terms
![](images/51cb9e8ffca0fbc51701b9e2fd2656c2062acee3ca657600e2d08b521046a141.jpg)

## Budget adds but not enough to budge GDP

Singapore<sup>’</sup>s FY2024 budget pencils in a budget deficit of 0.4% of GDP and an estimated fiscal impulse of 0.1% of GDP. The growth forecast is between 1-3%oya (JPMorgan: 0.7%oya) with a broadly neutral output gap. The general thrust of the budget focusses on households in the near term, especially the cost of living. In the longer term it provides more support for the SkillsFuture program which is focused on upskilling workers through educational and training grants. Under SkillsFuture, in recognition of the changing business environment, the budget provides for a temporary financial support scheme to support retrenched workers. This marks a notable change in support for labor redundancies.

For households, the budget via the assurance package has set aside S\$1.9 billion (0.3% of GDP) in transfers to manage the increased cost of living. In addition to the assurance package, for business, transfers to businesses via the Enterprise Support Package amount to S\$1.3billion (0.2% of GDP) to manage costs.

## January core CPI lower, February in focus

Singapore<sup>’</sup>s January core CPI (which excludes private road transport and accommodation) rose 0.4%m/m, sa and was up 3.1%oya, lower than expectations (J.P. Morgan and consensus: 3.6%oya). In trend sequential terms, core CPI rose 4.3%3m/3m, saar (Figure 5). Despite the 1% GST hike on January 1, core CPI rose 0.6%m/m, nsa compared to 0.8%m/ m, nsa last January following the initial 1% GST hike. One factor that could have affected pricing is the timing of the Lunar New Year holiday, which tends to see seasonal price increases, falling in January last year compared to February this year. Thus, despite the better-than-expected sequential outturn in core CPI, we are mindful that we could see a larger sequential increase in February given the risk of opportunistic pricing during the festive period.

Figure 5: Singapore core CPI %3m/3m, saar
![](images/c51cc5e5cef78043571d063db2b251d5aff4eef4e3cfcab21a973cc7a47c3a35.jpg)

Figure 6: Singapore core services %3m/3m, saar
![](images/6ef5f208a8bce496a0b7593db8d6909f53b58f427ab320ccb1a31fd62dae08e1.jpg)

## Caution the better part of valor

As noted, we had been concerned about the escalation in unit labor costs (ULC), especially services through much of 2023 (Figure 6). However, services ULC eased in 4Q23 and if this continues, would suggest that our prior concerns around the stickiness of labor costs should abate (Figure 7). The bulk of the slowdown came from payrolls rather than productivity and it remains to be seen whether the ongoing implementation of the Progressive Wage Model would impact labor costs this year (Figure 8). This is especially pertinent in sectors with a high share of labor inputs in their cost structure; retail trade, accommodation and food and beverage sectors.

Figure 7: Singapore core services ex travel and ULC %3m/3m, saar
![](images/0d2124a16b875a3301c35328da1b5f6653e998b5da092562b223f7aa1830816f.jpg)

Figure 8: Singapore unit labor cost
![](images/93bf5dca2318656a04259077e3bc5de4244b5f01f414c843645c6c28c3550776.jpg)

Although the ex-ante view of disinflation remains, the pace remains unclear given the residual uncertainties. This focuses attention on the sequential path of inflation rather than the over-year-ago comparisons. Uncertainties over pricing behavior suggest that monetary policy settings should be kept unchanged until there is clear evidence that inflation has indeed turned the corner, which should be evident in both input costs, via the DSPI (Domestic Supply Price Index), the ULC, and also latent pressures reflected in the pass-through or markup from supply-side costs into final prices. At this point, the evidence – especially ahead of the February CPI data – remains mixed.

## India

• Credit growth remains strong...

• …even amid first signs that RBI efforts to slow lending to some sectors is paying off

• Next week: February CPI at 5.1% oya and Jan IP at 3.7% oya

Bank credit continued to grow at a strong pace in the aggregate in January. That said, there are first signs that bank lending to non-bank financial companies (NBFCs) is slowing, reflecting the RBI<sup>’</sup>s efforts to slow unsecured lending. Next week, we expect February CPI to remain unchanged, at 5.1% oya. The details of the food basket will be keenly followed, as that could bear on the near-term inflation trajectory. Meanwhile, January IP is expected to remain unchanged, at 3.7% oya. While the tepid year-on-year January print masks a bounce in sequential momentum, IP growth has disappointed in recent months and is at odds with strong manufacturing growth in recent GDP prints.

## Bank credit growth remains strong

Bank credit growth remains strong, printing at 15.4% oya in January, similar to 15.6% in December (Figure 1). And the indication from fortnightly data is that it was even higher in February. The breakdown of bank credit data reveals that infrastructure and large industry credit growth, which saw strong sequential gains in December, gave back some of the gains in January. Credit growth in these sectors has continued to lag overall credit growth. Infrastructure clocked growth of 5.9% oya, while large industry posted 6.2% oya in January. Credit growth in January was again led by personal loans, which have been leading the charge for some time.

Figure 1: Bank Credit growth
![](images/c1dcc38a6723b6882797541918f34d6417543039db187337063bfa064105b960.jpg)

Personal loans again grew at a strong 1.7% m/m, sa in January, translating into 21.1% oya (Figure 2). The personal loan category includes both secured (housing, vehicles) and unsecured loans (credit cards). The RBI has been raising concerns about strong growth in unsecured loans like credit cards. Credit card loan growth remained strong in January, coming in at 31.2% oya. Secured loans like housing also continue to grow at a robust pace, reflecting an upturn in the housing cycle. However, bank lending to NBFCs fell sequentially for a second consecutive month, such that year-on-year growth slowed to 15.6% in January from 21.5% oya in November. Recall that the RBI has imposed higher risk weights on lending to NBFCs in a bid to slow unsecured lending, and there are first signs that this may have begun to take hold.

Figure 2: Personal Loans
![](images/bf3fb3d2bb255b4fc9ef30ea374b97f55398a82938f363ffc7efdfbd26064290.jpg)

## February CPI expected at 5.1% oya

We expect February CPI to come in at 5.1% oya next week, unchanged from January. In sequential terms, this would mean growth of 0.4% m/m, sa vs. 0.5% in January. The softening in food prices has been modest, with vegetables and pulses witnessing only a minor fall. Core-core inflation (core ex petrol and diesel) is expected to come in at 0.3% m/m, sa, the same as last month, which would translate into a 3.6% year-on-year print – a record low in the series (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Food and core-core inflation
![](images/7931543905826423fb83ca60974f32134712714ae4b40abdfd6966c765c47eda.jpg)

## January IP expected at 3.7% oya

We expect IP to print at 3.7% oya next week, unchanged from 3.8% in January. This would mean sequentially strong 2.6% growth underpinned by robust electricity and auto production data. That said, IP growth has been lackluster in recent months. This is at odds with strong manufacturing growth in the GDP data. The latter is computed as a weighted average of IP growth and the operating profits of manufacturers. Manufacturing growth printed a strong 11.6% oya in the 4Q23

GDP report, even as manufacturing IP growth in the quarter was just 5% oya (Figure 4). The wedge is because of strong manufacturing operating profits, reflecting lower year-onyear cost growth, as commodity prices have softened compared to a year ago. Despite tepid IP growth, therefore, how manufacturing firm operating profits evolve will have an important bearing on the GDP growth forecast.

Figure 4: GVA manufacturing and IP manufacturing
![](images/a828d29c7a277d0dfbe6487f1077d420b4ee66f9aeeec5b7dc95fd9d8d9cc311.jpg)

## Asia focus: Population aging

Since the 2000s, Asia has experienced rapid population aging. Korea and Thailand's median age surpassed that of the OECD in 2010 and 2020, respectively. Over the past two decades, Korea has seen the steepest increase in the median age from 32 to 44 years (Figure 1). China and Vietnam, although still younger than the OECD average, have seen their populations age by eight years, narrowing the gap with OECD. This aging is largely due to low fertility rates, falling below the replacement level of 2 well before the 2000s and failing to rebound since then (Figure 2).

Figure 1: Median age in Asia
![](images/2b6b9c0ce9a81d2f76743f0c546934fbde46eb160274da6cf10f519b23b3e1a6.jpg)
Source: UN Population Prospects (2022) and J.P. Morgan

Figure 2: Total fertility rate in Asia
![](images/3ce78692c8a48a822309e9a861b6f934f2106a79770fe00673cba4b0d935418a.jpg)
Source: UN Population Prospects (2022) and J.P. Morgan

Korea and Singapore have increased public spending on family benefits, but they have not succeeded in boosting fertility (Figure 3). In 2022, only India, Indonesia, and the Philippines had fertility rates above 2 (Figure 2). The aging trend is expected to accelerate, with UN projections suggesting that Singapore will have only 2 working-age persons supporting each senior by 2042 (and even fewer in Japan, Korea, and Hong Kong), a sharp decrease from 10 in 2002 and the current 5 (Figure 4). To manage aging impacts, strategies could include improving technology (to compensate for reduced labor contribution to growth) or increasing national saving to fund future elderly support. Japan's experience offers a model, as it spent 2.6% of GDP on research and development and accumulated foreign assets through current account surpluses when it reached a peak in its working-age population in 1994 (Table 1). Hong Kong and Singapore show parallels to Japan in these respects, while South Korea can leverage its high R&D investment for productivity gains. China and Thailand couldn<sup>’</sup>t join the high-income group when their demographics were most favorable and currently exhibit lower fertility rates compared to their income levels (Figure 5).

Figure 3: Policy responses to low fertility
![](images/f97225c49fc2cf809652255375d7e347eef90bd694dc417d94167fc323e1a62d.jpg)

Figure 4: Old-age dependency ratio
No. of those aged 65+, per 100 people of aged 15-64
![](images/c00d5ac87a00fcb1a741aef4789d1007c7dc9478f53cab2808835dfd2a427186.jpg)
